1. Sparse Matrix formats

Peter Kandolf

Research Area Scientific Computing - University of Innsbruck

Spares matrix formats

🎯 Learning goals of this lecture

  • what are spares matrices
  • How to deal with sparse matrices in an efficient way

Why Sparse?

  • Many real-world problems produce matrices with \(\ll 1\%\) nonzeros: PDEs, graphs, ML, optimization.
  • Storage and time: dense \(\mathcal{O}(n^2)\) memory vs. sparse \(\mathcal{O}(\text{nnz})\).
  • Computation: operations like SpMV scale as \(\mathcal{O}(\text{nnz})\).
  • No clear line to separate sparse from dense/full matrices.
  • Operational definition: choosing a specific sparse storage scheme, do I (compared to the full storage scheme) get
    • increased performance?
    • lower memory consumption?

Key Operations

  • Sparse matrix–vector multiply (SpMV): \(y \leftarrow A x\)
  • Triangular solves with sparse factors
  • Sparse matrix–matrix product (SpGEMM): \(C \leftarrow A B\)
  • Preconditioned iterative methods: \(M^{-1} A x = M^{-1} b\)

Data structures for sparse matrices

The purpose of a sparse matrix storage format is to reduce the amount of memory that is required to hold that matrix in memory → data structures, that do not need to store all the non-zero entries. This usually also has a positive effect on performance as the zero-entries do not contribute to the result of, say, a matrix-vector multiplication (i.e., we do not perform the corresponding floating point operations).

The banded matrix store format (gb) does exactly that for a particular type of sparse matrices (namely banded matrices): the amount of storage required is proportional to the number of non-zero entries in the matrix (as opposed to the number of entries in the matrix).

We are now looking at storage formats that can store arbitrary matrices in such a manner, that the amount of storage required is proportional to the number of non-zero entries.(dense matrices could be stored in these formats as well, but this would be quite inefficient).

Overview - Coordinate (COO)

  • Triplet lists \((i,j,a_{ij})\).
  • Advantages: easy to build, insert, concatenate.
  • Disadvantages: slow arithmetic; need sorting and duplicate summation.

Overview - Compressed Sparse Row (CSR)

  • Arrays: rowptr (size \(m+1\)), colind (size nnz), values (size nnz).
  • Row \(i\) stored in indices \(k \in [\texttt{rowptr}[i], \texttt{rowptr}[i+1])\).
  • SpMV: for each row \(i\), for \(k\) in row-range, accumulate \(y_i = y_i + a_k x_{\text{colind}[k]}\). Cost \(\approx 2\cdot\text{nnz}\) flops.

Overview - Compressed Sparse Column (CSC)

  • Column-major analog of CSR: colptr, rowind, values.
  • Natural for column operations and some factorization libraries.

Overview - Other Formats (When to Use)

  • ELL/ELLPACK: fixed nonzeros per row; good on GPUs for uniform sparsity.
  • HYB (ELL+COO): hybrid for irregular sparsity (e.g., cuSPARSE).
  • BCSR: block compressed row for block-structured matrices.
  • DIA: diagonal storage for banded matrices.

See https://netlib.org/linalg/html_templates/node90.html for a discussion of some formats by Jack Dongarra.

The compressed row storage (CSR) format

This format requires three different arrays:

double* val; 
int* col_ind; 
int* row_ptr;
  • val stores the values of the non-zero entries. The other two arrays determine the position of the according values:
  • col_ind store the column index of the corresponding entry in val. It has the same size as val (the number of non-zero elements of the matrix).
  • row_ptr could do the same as col_ind for the rows, but that would not be as memory efficient. It only stores the index in val at which a new row starts → its size is qual to the number of rows \(+ 1\) of the matrix (which is almost always smaller than the number of non-zero elements).

CSR format: example

\[A=\begin{bmatrix} a_{11} & 0 & 0 & a_{14} & 0 \\ 0 & 0 & 0 & 0 & 0\\ 0 & 0 & a_{33} & a_{34} & a_{35}\\ 0 & 0 & 0 & a_{44} & 0 \\ a_{51} & 0 & 0 & 0 & a_{55} \end{bmatrix} \]

is stored as follows:

val = {a11, a14, a33, a34, a35, a44, a51, a55}; 
col_ind = {0, 3, 2, 3, 4, 3, 0, 4}; 
row_ptr = {0, 2, 2, 5, 6, 8};

CSR format

  • The CSR format is widely supported and the de facto standard if no more appropriate format is available for a given problem.
  • There is no issue in finding software that converts to and from CSR stored matrices.
  • It is very difficult to construct a matrix for which the number of non-zero entries in each row are not known beforehand (the assembly step of certain finite element methods would, e.g., require this).

CSR format: strategies for assembling 1

  • First construct the matrix in a different format which is then converted to CSR
  • only useful if the construction step is not the performance limiting part of the numerical simulation

CSR format: strategies for assembling 2

  • Use the spares storage format coordinate list (COO), which stores three arrays with the value of the non-zero entry, the column index, and the row index of this entry:

    double* val = {a11, a33, a34, a44, a51, a55, a14, a35}; 
int* col_ind = {0, 2, 3, 3, 0, 4, 3, 4}; 
int* row_ind = {0, 2, 2, 3, 4, 4, 0, 2};

  • format duplicates some information

  • allowed elements can appear in any order

  • particularly easy to construct a matrix

After sorting the elements by row

double* val = {a11, a14, a33, a34, a35, a44, a51, a55}; 
int* col_ind = {0, 3, 2, 3, 4, 3, 0, 4}; 
int* row_ind = {0, 0, 2, 2, 2, 3, 4, 4};

it is straight forward to convert this format to CSR (which is usually more efficient in terms of storage as well as with respect to performance).

CSR format: sparse matrix-vector multiplication

for (int i = 0; i<N; i++) {
  y[i] = 0.0;
  // iterate over non-zeros
  for(int j = row_ptr[i]; j<row_ptr[i+1];j++)
    y[i] += val[j]*x[col_ind[j]];
}
  • The outer loop iterates over all rows.
  • The inner loop processes the columns that have non-zero values.

Sparse matrix libraries 1/2

Implementing certain dense matrix routines can be already a challenging task if optimal performance is desired. This is even more true for implementations based on sparse matrix formats.

We have repeatedly emphasized that sequential memory access is important for good performance. However, if we transverse a CSR stored sparse matrix sequentially in order to perform a matrix-vector multiplication, there is no guarantee that the corresponding access into the vector are sequential. This usually results in poor cache performance and thus in significant loss of performance. Fortunately, a number of sparse matrix libraries exist which have been extensively optimized.

Sparse matrix libraries 2/2

  • Libraries that implement the BLAS functionality of sparse matrices: SuiteSparse, PSBLAS, certain vendor implementations (such as Intel MKL)
  • Libraries that implement entire linear solvers (both direct and iterative methods)
    • PETSc & Trilinos: implement iterative methods and preconditioners
    • MUMPS: implements direct solvers that are optimized for sparse matrices
  • eigen

eigen - Building Sparse Matrices

#include <Eigen/Sparse>
#include <vector>
using T = double;
using SpMat = Eigen::SparseMatrix<T>;     // column-major by default
using Triplet = Eigen::Triplet<T>;

SpMat build_matrix(int m, int n, const std::vector<Triplet>& trips) {
  SpMat A(m,n);
  A.setFromTriplets(trips.begin(), trips.end()); // sums duplicates
  A.makeCompressed(); // optional: ensure compressed form
  return A;
}
  • Use Eigen::SparseMatrix<T, RowMajor> for CSR-like traversal.
  • Prefer bulk insertion via triplets; avoid repeated coefficient writes in hot loops.

eigen - SpMV and Basic Ops

#include <Eigen/Sparse>
#include <Eigen/Dense>
int main(){
  using SpMat = Eigen::SparseMatrix<double>;
  Eigen::VectorXd x, y;
  SpMat A; // assume filled and compressed
  y = A * x;               // SpMV
  SpMat B = A.transpose(); // cheap transpose view (explicit if needed)
  SpMat C = A + B;         // addition (may uncompress/realloc)
}

Performance tips:

  • Ensure A.isCompressed() and OuterIndexPtr monotone.
  • Reserve nonzeros when building: A.reserve(Eigen::VectorXi::Constant(n, k)).

SuiteSparse Overview

  • A collection of high-quality sparse matrix packages by T. A. Davis et al.
  • Key components:
    • CHOLMOD: sparse Cholesky for symmetric positive definite (SPD).
    • UMFPACK: sparse LU for general (unsymmetric) matrices.
    • SPQR: multifrontal sparse QR (rank-revealing, least squares).
    • Ordering: AMD/CAMD, COLAMD/CCOLAMD; interfaces to METIS/ParMETIS.
  • Strengths:
    • Robust, production-ready direct solvers with fill-reducing orderings.
    • Supports real/complex, single/double; threaded via OpenMP in some builds.
    • Interoperates with BLAS/LAPACK; well-integrated in many ecosystems (MATLAB, Eigen, Python, Julia).
  • Website: https://people.engr.tamu.edu/davis/suitesparse.html

MKL Sparse Overview 1/2

  • Intel MKL provides:
    • Sparse BLAS C interface: inspector–executor API for CSR/CSC/COO.
    • PARDISO: high-performance direct solver (LU/LDLT) for sparse systems.
    • RCI iterative solvers: CG, FGMRES, BiCGSTAB with preconditioning.
  • Typical workflow (Sparse BLAS):
    • Create sparse handle from CSR/COO via mkl_sparse_*_create_csr/coo.
    • Inspect and optimize pattern via mkl_sparse_optimize.
    • Execute operations: SpMV (mkl_sparse_d_mv), SpMM (mkl_sparse_d_mm), triangular solves, etc.

MKL Sparse Overview 2/2

  • Advantages:
    • Preprocessing captures sparsity patterns for faster repeated calls.
    • Threaded and vectorized kernels; supports symmetric structure and hints.
  • Docs: https://www.intel.com/content/www/us/en/developer/tools/oneapi/onemkl-documentation.html

Example - eigen with SuiteSparse cholmod

Code file eigen_colmod.cpp

// src/eigen_cholmod.cpp
#include <iostream>
#include <Eigen/Sparse>
#include <Eigen/CholmodSupport> // requires CHOLMOD installed

int main() {
  using SpMat = Eigen::SparseMatrix<double>;
  SpMat A(3,3);
  A.insert(0,0)=4; A.insert(0,1)=-1;
  A.insert(1,0)=-1; A.insert(1,1)=4; A.insert(1,2)=-1;
  A.insert(2,1)=-1; A.insert(2,2)=3;
  Eigen::Vector3d b(1,2,3);

  Eigen::CholmodSimplicialLDLT<SpMat> solver;
  solver.compute(A);
  if(solver.info()!=Eigen::Success) {
    std::cerr << "Factorization failed\n";
    return 1;
  }
  Eigen::Vector3d x = solver.solve(b);
  std::cout << "x = " << x.transpose() << "\n";
  return 0;
}

Example - eigen with SuiteSparse cholmod - build script

Code file CMakeLists.txt

cmake_minimum_required(VERSION 3.10)
project(Example VERSION 1.0 LANGUAGES C CXX Fortran)

set(CMAKE_CXX_STANDARD 17)
set(CMAKE_CXX_STANDARD_REQUIRED True)

set(CBLAS True)
include(FetchContent)
FetchContent_Declare(
    lapack
    GIT_REPOSITORY https://github.com/Reference-LAPACK/lapack/
    GIT_TAG v3.12.1
    DOWNLOAD_EXTRACT_TIMESTAMP TRUE
)
FetchContent_MakeAvailable(lapack)

set(BLA_VENDOR NETLIB_BLAS)
# Tell SuiteSparse to use the BLAS/LAPACK we just built
set(SUITESPARSE_USE_EXTERNAL_BLAS_LAPACK ON CACHE BOOL "" FORCE)
# The LAPACK build defines plain targets "blas" and "lapack"
set(BLAS_LIBRARIES   blas  CACHE STRING "" FORCE)
set(LAPACK_LIBRARIES lapack CACHE STRING "" FORCE)
set(SUITESPARSE_ENABLE_PROJECTS "cholmod" CACHE STRING "" FORCE)

FetchContent_Declare(
    SuiteSparse
    GIT_REPOSITORY https://github.com/DrTimothyAldenDavis/SuiteSparse
    GIT_TAG v7.14.1
    DOWNLOAD_EXTRACT_TIMESTAMP TRUE
)
FetchContent_MakeAvailable(SuiteSparse)
FetchContent_Declare(
    eigen
    GIT_REPOSITORY https://gitlab.com/libeigen/eigen
    GIT_TAG 5.0
    DOWNLOAD_EXTRACT_TIMESTAMP TRUE
)
FetchContent_MakeAvailable(eigen)

# Add the executable
add_executable(main src/eigen_colmod.cpp)
target_include_directories(main PRIVATE ${suitesparse_SOURCE_DIR}/CHOLMOD/Include)
target_link_libraries(main PRIVATE lapack SuiteSparse::CHOLMOD Eigen3::Eigen)
target_compile_options(main PRIVATE -Wall -Wextra -Wpedantic)
